\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)\\
\partial_{\theta}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\sigma\sigma}\varphi(x,x,\pm 1,0)&\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi(x,x,\pm 1,0)\\
\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi(x,x,\pm 1,0)&\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi(x,x,\pm 1,0)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)\\
\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)\end{pmatrix}=0.

Now, we have already proved that

(A.20)

\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)=0

in (
A.1
). Moreover, we know that, for
\Theta>0
,

(A.21)

q_{1}(\sigma,\pm\Theta,0)=\Theta q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0).

Hence,

(A.22)

\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)=q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)\neq 0,

where we use the
ellipticity
of
q_{1}
given by (
2.1
). Finally, observe that

(A.23)

q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)=G(p_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0),\pm 1)

doesn’t depend on
\theta
since
p_{1}
doesn’t depend on
\theta
((
2.28
)). Hence,

(A.24)

\partial_{\theta}q_{1}(\sigma,\pm 1,0)=0.

Now, (
A.19
), (
A.20
),(
A.22
),(
A.24
) ensure altogether that

(A.25)

\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi(x,x,\pm 1,0)=0.

Since, moreover, there holds that, thanks to (
A.3
),

(A.26)

\partial_{\sigma}q_{1}(0,\pm 1,0)=0,

(
A.19
), (
A.20
),(
A.22
),(
A.24
) also imply (
A.16
).
∎

Lemma A.4

(The third
(\theta,w)
derivatives of
\varphi
)
.

There holds

(A.27)

\begin{cases}\partial_{\theta\theta\theta}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)&=-\frac{\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q(\sigma,1,0)(\partial_{\sigma}q(\sigma,1,0))^{2}}{(\partial_{\Theta}q(\sigma,1,0))^{3}}\\
\partial_{\theta\theta w}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)&=-\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\frac{\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q(\sigma,1,0)\partial_{\sigma}q(\sigma,0,1)}{(\partial_{\Theta}q(\sigma,1,0))^{2}}\\
\partial_{\theta ww}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)&=-\left(\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\frac{\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))}{\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))}\\
\partial_{www}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)&=0.\end{cases}

Proof.

First, using (
3.35
), it is obvious that, for all
w
, there holds

(A.28)

\partial_{www}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)=0.

Second, still using (
3.35
), there holds

(A.29)

\partial_{\theta ww}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)=\langle v_{H},g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)\rangle,

where
v_{H}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}
.

Since the curve
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
defined by (
4.11
) is symmetric,
v_{H}
and
g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)
are colinear (indeed,
g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
at
g_{\sigma}(0)
). Moreover, looking at the direction of
g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)
, there holds

(A.30)

\langle v_{H},g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)\rangle=-|g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)|.

Now, let us differentiate two times in
w
the equation

(A.31)

q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(w))=1,

and evaluate at
w=0
. This yields

(A.32)

\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))\cdot g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)+g_{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)\cdot\nabla_{\xi}^{2}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))\cdot g_{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)=0.

Since moreover there holds in coordinates
\Theta,\Sigma
that

(A.33)

\begin{split}&g_{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\end{pmatrix}\\
&g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}-|g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)|\\
0\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

equation (
A.32
) reduces to

(A.34)

-\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))|g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)|+\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))\left(\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}=0,

which obviously yields, using (
A.29
) and (
A.30
), that

(A.35)

\begin{split}\partial_{\theta ww}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)&=-|g_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}(0)|\\
&=-\left(\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\frac{\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))}{\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))}.\end{split}

Third, in order to compute
\partial_{\theta\theta w}\varphi
, we can no longer rely on (
3.35
). Instead, we have to resort to the eikonal equation (
3.34
), similarly to the proof of Lemma
A.3
. Using (
A.19
), we write

(A.36)

\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi((t,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),w)\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,\nabla_{x}\varphi)+\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi((t,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),g_{\sigma}(w))\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,\nabla_{x}\varphi))=0.

Now, we want to differentiate this equality in
w
, and to evaluate at
w=0
. Since we already know, thanks to Lemmas
A.1
and
A.3
, that

(A.37)

\begin{split}&\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))=0\\
&\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),g_{\sigma}(0))=0,\end{split}

we can already see, using moreover (
3.35
) that differentiating equation (
A.36
) in
w
, and evaluating at
w=0
, will yield

(A.38)

\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)\left(\partial_{\Sigma}\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))\right)\cdot g_{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)+\partial_{\theta\theta w}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))=0.

Now, using (
A.19
), one finds that

(A.39)

\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)=-\frac{\partial_{\sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))}{\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))}.

Moreover, by definition, there holds in coordinates
(\Theta,\Sigma)
that

(A.40)

g_{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence,

(A.41)

\left(\partial_{\Sigma}\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))\right)\cdot g_{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0)).

Thus, equations (
A.38
), (
A.39
), and (
A.41
), yield together that

(A.42)

\partial_{\theta\theta w}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),g_{\sigma}(0))=-\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\frac{\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q(\sigma,1,0)\partial_{\sigma}q(\sigma,1,0)}{(\partial_{\Theta}q(\sigma,1,0))^{2}}.

Fourth, in order to compute
\partial_{\theta\theta\theta}\varphi
, we have no choice but to iterate the implicit argument of Lemma
A.3
. We can actually start from (
A.36
). We want to differentiate this equation, and evaluate it at
t=0
. Using again (
A.37
), and (
3.35
), there holds

(A.43)

\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi\left(\partial_{\Sigma}\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}\right)\cdot\partial_{\theta}\nabla_{x}\varphi+\partial_{\theta\theta\theta}\varphi\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}=0,

where all the quantities involving
\varphi
are taken at
(0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),g_{\sigma}(0)
, and all the quantities involving
q_{1}
are taken at
(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))
. Since there holds moreover that, thanks to Lemma
A.3
,

(A.44)

\partial_{\theta}\nabla_{x}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),g_{\sigma}(0))=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi\end{pmatrix},

the equation (
A.43
) reduces to

(A.45)

(\partial_{\sigma\theta}\varphi)^{2}\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}+\partial_{\theta\theta\theta}\varphi\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}=0,

where again, all the quantities involving
\varphi
are taken at
(0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),g_{\sigma}(0)
and all the quantities involving
q_{1}
are taken at
(\sigma,g_{\sigma}(0))
. One can then conclude, using (
A.39
), that

(A.46)

\partial_{\theta\theta\theta}\varphi((0,\sigma,),(0,\sigma),0)=-\frac{\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q(\sigma,1,0)(\partial_{\sigma}q(\sigma,1,0))^{2}}{(\partial_{\Theta}q(\sigma,1,0))^{3}}.

∎

A.3
A technical lemma

In this section, we state and prove the following simple lemma.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval, let
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I)
, and let
a\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(I)
. Assume that

(A.47)

\text{the stationary points of}\ \phi\ \text{are isolated}.

Define the oscillatory integral

(A.48)

\mathcal{I}(\lambda):=\int_{I}e^{i\lambda\phi(x)}a(x)dx.

There holds

(A.49)

\mathcal{I}(\lambda)\to 0,\hskip 18.49988pt\lambda\to\infty.

Proof.

Because the stationary points of
\phi
are isolated, there is a finite number of stationary points in the support of
\phi
, say
x_{1},...,x_{N}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
I_{1},...,I_{N}
be intervals of size
\varepsilon
centered at
x_{1},...,x_{N}
respectively. Then, on the one hand, for
i=1,...,N
,

(A.50)

\left|\int_{I_{i}}e^{i\lambda\phi(x)}a(x)dx\right|\leqslant\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\varepsilon.

On the other hand, since
\phi
is not stationary on
J:=I\backslash(I_{1}\cup...\cup I_{N})
, there holds

(A.51)

\int_{J}e^{i\lambda\phi(x)}a(x)dx\to 0,\hskip 18.49988pt\lambda\to\infty.

Overall, there holds

(A.52)

\limsup_{\lambda\to\infty}|\mathcal{I}(\lambda)|\leqslant N\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\varepsilon.

This concludes the proof since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
2.5

B.1
First part of the proposition

We first prove the first part of the proposition, that is the fact that, if

(B.1)

\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\hskip 18.49988ptq_{1}(x,\xi)\leqslant 1\}

is strictly convex, then
\mathcal{S}
satisfie the non intersection of bicharacteristic curve Hypothesis
2.3
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{S}
, and fix two distinct directions
(x,\xi)\neq(x,\eta)\in S_{x}^{*}\mathcal{S}
. We want to prove that
t\mapsto\Phi^{q_{1}}_{t}(x,\xi)
and
s\mapsto\Phi^{q_{1}}_{s}(x,\eta)
do not intersect outside of
\{x,\bar{x}\}
. Assume that this is false, i.e. that there exists
y\notin\{x,\bar{x}\}
at which those two curves intersect. Then,
x
cannot be one of the Poles, since, thanks to Lemma
2.5
, the bicharacteristic curves of
q_{1}
passing through the Poles are exactly the meridians, which don’t intersect outside of the Poles.

Now, using the periodicity of the flow of
q_{1}
, and up to changing
\xi
or
\eta
or both into their opposite, we can assume without loss of generality that there exist
0<s,t<\pi
such that

(B.2)

P(\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi))=P(\Phi_{s}^{q_{1}}(x,\eta))=y.

From the explicit formula (
2.5
), we can further reduce, up to exchanging
x
and
\bar{x}
, and thanks to the symmetries of
\mathcal{S}
, to the following situation : on the one hand
(x,\xi)
and
(x,\eta)
both point towards the North, i.e. for some Clairaut integrals
I\neq J\in[-1,1]
there holds

(B.3)

\begin{split}(x,\xi)&=\left(\theta,\sigma,I,\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}I^{2}}\right)\\
(x,\eta)&=\left(\theta,\sigma,J,\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}J^{2}}\right).\end{split}

On the other hand, this remains true along the trajectories between
x
and
y
, i.e. the tangent to
u\mapsto\Phi_{u}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi)
(resp
u\mapsto\Phi_{u}^{q_{1}}(x,\eta)
) points towards the North for
u\in[0,t]
(resp
u\in[0,s]
). This is the situation of Figure
28
. The important assumption is that the direction of travel (pointing the North or South Pole) don’t strictly change on the portion of trajectory that we are interested in. We moreover assume that
y
is the
first
intersection of the bicharacteristic curves, i.e. they don’t intersect on the portion
[x,y]
of their trajectories.

Figure 28:
Intersection of bicharacteristic curves outside of the antipodal refocalisation

The point is that we can thus find the following explicit formula, using the conservation of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
by the Hamiltonian flow, and the conservation of the sign of the
\Sigma
coordinate, thanks to our reduction. Writing
y=(\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\sigma})
, there holds

(B.4)

\begin{split}\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi)&=\left(\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\sigma},I,\sqrt{1-f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}I^{2}}\right)\\
\Phi_{s}^{q_{1}}(x,\eta)&=\left(\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\sigma},J,\sqrt{1-f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}J^{2}}\right).\end{split}

In particular, the angle between the directions of
\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi)
and
\Phi_{s}^{q_{1}}(x,\eta)
is of the
same sign
as the angle between
(x,\xi)
and
(x,\eta)
. Precisely, there holds

(B.5)

\det(\begin{pmatrix}
I \\
\sqrt{1 - f(\sigma)^{-2}I^2}
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}
J \\
\sqrt{1 - f(\sigma)^{-2}J^2}
\end{pmatrix})\det(\begin{pmatrix}
I \\
\sqrt{1 - f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}I^2}
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}
J\\
\sqrt{1 - f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}J^2}
\end{pmatrix})>0,

or, more geometrically, seen as half-line of the upper half-plane of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in coordinates
(\Theta,\Sigma)
, the directions
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}I\\
\sqrt{1-f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}I^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}J\\
\sqrt{1-f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}J^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
are in the same order than the directions
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}I\\
\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}I^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}J\\
\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}J^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
in the sense of Figure
29
.

Figure 29:
The conservation of the sign of the angle along trajectories

Now, in sharp contrast, there is an
inversion
of the sign of the angle between the directions of the velocity vectors of the bicharacteristic curves between
x
and
y
. Precisely, if we denote
v_{I}\in S_{x}\mathcal{S}
(resp
v_{J}
) the velocity of the bicharacteristic curve
t\mapsto P(\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi))
(resp
s\mapsto P(\Phi_{s}^{q_{1}}(x,\eta))
) at
x
, and
\tilde{v}_{I}\in S_{y}\mathcal{S}
(resp
\tilde{v}_{J}
) the velocity of the bicharacteristic curve
t\mapsto P(\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi))
(resp
s\mapsto P(\Phi_{s}^{q_{1}}(x,\eta))
) at
y
, there holds

(B.6)

\det(v_I, v_J)\det(\tilde{v}_I, \tilde{v}_J)<0,

which can be read from Figure
28
(we use here the fact that
y
is the first point of intersection along the bicharacteristic curves, and the fact that, thanks to our assumptions, the bicharacteristic curves point towards the North on all of the portion
[x,y]
).

Now, using the formula for the velocity vector along the Hamiltonian flow of a function on the cotangent bundle, projected on the manifold, i.e. (
2.10
), we can
compute
the velocity vectors as follows

(B.7)

\begin{split}&v_{I}=\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}\left(\sigma,I,\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}I^{2}}\right)\\
&v_{J}=\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}\left(\sigma,J,\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}J^{2}}\right)\\
&\tilde{v}_{I}=\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}\left(\tilde{\sigma},I,\sqrt{1-f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}I^{2}}\right)\\
&\tilde{v}_{J}=\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}\left(\tilde{\sigma},J,\sqrt{1-f(\tilde{\sigma})^{-2}J^{2}}\right).\end{split}

Hence, from (
B.7
) and (
B.6
), and comparing with (
B.5
), we can conclude to a contradiction provided we prove the fundamental fact that, for all
\sigma
and for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}=(\Theta,\Sigma),(\tilde{\Theta},\tilde{\Sigma})
in the upper half-plane (i.e.
\Sigma,\tilde{\Sigma}\geqslant 0
) there holds that the angle between
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
has the
same sign
than the angle between
\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(\sigma,\xi_{1})
and
\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(\sigma,\tilde{\xi_{2}})
i.e.

(B.8)

\det(\xi_1,\xi_2)\det(\nabla_{\xi}q_1(\sigma,\xi_1), \nabla_{\xi} q_1(\sigma,\xi_2))\geqslant 0.

Now, for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the direction of
\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(\sigma,\xi)
is simply the normal direction to the curve
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}:=\{q_{1}=1\}
at its intersection point with
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\xi
, see Figure
30
.

Figure 30:
Visualisation of
\nabla_{\xi}q_{1}(x,\xi)

Hence, equation (
B.8
) is simply a
convexity
equation, i.e. it means that the domain
\{q_{1}\leqslant 1\}
is
convex
(at least in the upper half-plane), see Figure
31
. This concludes the proof of the first part of Proposition
2.5
.
∎

Figure 31:
The convexity of
\{q_{1}\leqslant 1\}

B.2
Second part of the proposition

We now prove the second part of the proposition.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+}
be the intersection between the curve
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
and the strict upper right quadrant of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, i.e.

(B.9)

\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\sigma}=\{(\Theta,\Sigma)\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*})^{2}\hskip 18.49988ptq_{1}(\sigma,\Theta,\Sigma)=1\}.

We first claim that this curve has an everywhere non zero curvature in the two cases of the proposition. In order to prove that fact, we introduce a parameterization of
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+}
which may seem odd, but which will help greatly with the computations.

First, let
\mathcal{E}
be the intersection of the curve
\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\ p_{1}(\sigma,\xi)=1\}
with the strict upper right quadrant of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, i.e.

(B.10)

\mathcal{E}:=\{\xi\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*})^{2}\hskip 18.49988ptp_{1}(\sigma,\xi)=1\}.

Then, since
q_{1}
is a smooth non vanishing homogeneous function of degree 1, there is a one-to-one correspondence say

(B.11)

A\in\mathcal{E}\mapsto h(A)\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+},

Hence, we need only parameterize the curve
\mathcal{E}
. Since we are in the strict upper right quadrant, we can always parameterize it by
p_{2}\mapsto A(p_{2})
(recall that
p_{2}(\sigma,\Theta,\Sigma)=\Theta
), i.e.

(B.12)

\mathcal{E}=\left\{(p_{2},\sqrt{1-f^{-2}(\sigma)p_{2}^{2}},\hskip 18.49988ptp_{2}\in(0,f(\sigma))\right\}.

Ultimately, we have a one-to-one parameterization of the curve
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+}
by
p_{2}\in(0,f(\sigma))
, say
p_{2}\mapsto h(A(p_{2}))
. Now, observe that, for all
A\in\mathcal{E}
,

(B.13)

\frac{\nabla q_{1}}{|\nabla q_{1}|}(h(A))=\frac{\nabla q_{1}}{|\nabla q_{1}|}(A),

since
\frac{\nabla q_{1}}{|\nabla q_{1}|}
is a homogeneous function of degree 0 on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash(0,0)
and since
A
and
h(A)
are positively colinear by construction. Hence, in order to prove the claim, we need only prove that the function

(B.14)

p_{2}\in(0,f(\sigma))\mapsto\frac{\nabla q_{1}}{|\nabla q_{1}|}(A(p_{2}))

has an everywhere nonzero derivative.

Now, the interest of this reduction is that, thanks to Lemma
2.4
, we have a simple expression of
\nabla q_{1}
on
\mathcal{E}
. Indeed, observe that this lemma yields that

(B.15)

q_{1}(\sigma,\Theta,\Sigma)=G(p_{1}(\sigma,\Theta,\Sigma),\Theta)=p_{1}g\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right),

where for
I\in(0,1)
,
g
is the function introduced by Bleher in
[
7
]
[Equation (6.3)], i.e.

(B.16)

g(I)=I+\pi^{-1}\int_{\sigma_{-}(I)}^{\sigma_{+}(I)}\sqrt{1-f(s)^{-2}I^{2}}ds.

In particular, since
\partial_{\Sigma}p_{2}=0
and
\partial_{\Theta}p_{2}=1
we find that

(B.17)

\nabla q_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\Theta}q_{1}\\
\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\left[g\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right)-\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}g^{\prime}\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right)\right]\partial_{\Theta}p_{1}+g^{\prime}\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right)\\
\left[g\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right)-\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}g^{\prime}\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right)\right]\partial_{\Sigma}p_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

Now, on
\mathcal{E}
, there holds
p_{1}=1
. In particular, using (
2.17
), there holds

(B.18)

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\Theta}p_{1}\\
\partial_{\Sigma}p_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f(\sigma)^{-2}p_{2}\\
\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}p_{2}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, on
\mathcal{E}
, there holds

(B.19)

\nabla q_{1}(A(p_{2}))=\begin{pmatrix}\left[g(p_{2})-p_{2}g^{\prime}(p_{2})\right]f(\sigma)^{-2}p_{2}+g^{\prime}(p_{2})\\
[g(p_{2})-p_{2}g^{\prime}(p_{2})]\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^{-2}p_{2}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, using the local notations
f:=f(\sigma)
and
p:=p_{2}
, what we want is to prove that the function

(B.20)

p\in(0,f)\mapsto\frac{\nabla q_{1}}{|\nabla q_{1}|}(A(p))=\frac{1}{|\nabla q_{1}|(A(p))}\begin{pmatrix}[g(p)-pg^{\prime}(p)]f^{-2}p+g^{\prime}(p)\\
[g(p)-pg^{\prime}(p)]\sqrt{1-f^{-2}p^{2}}\end{pmatrix}

has a nonzero derivative. Now, since it is a function which takes value in the circle, this is equivalent to proving that, for all
p\in(0,f)
,

(B.21)

\det(\nabla q_1(A(p)), \frac{d}{d p} \left(\nabla q_1 (A(p))\right))\neq 0.

Using the explicit expression (
B.19
), this is equivalent to proving that

(B.22)

\det(\begin{pmatrix}
[g - pg'] f^{-2} p + g' \\
[g - pg']\sqrt{1 - f^{-2} p^2}
\end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix}
- p g'' f^{-2} p + [g - pg'] f^{-2} + g'' \\
-pg''\sqrt{1 - f^{-2}p^2} -\frac{[g-pg']f^{-2} p}{\sqrt{1 - f^{-2} p^2}}
\end{pmatrix})

doesn’t vanish, where we don’t note the variable
p
in the argument of the functions
g,g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}
. Multiplying the second line by
\sqrt{1-f^{-2}p^{2}}
, this is again equivalent to proving that, for
p\in(0,f)

(B.23)

\begin{vmatrix}[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}p+g^{\prime}&g^{\prime\prime}(1-f^{-2}p^{2})+[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}\\
[g-pg^{\prime}](1-f^{-2}p^{2})&-pg^{\prime\prime}(1-f^{-2}p^{2})-[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}p\end{vmatrix}\neq 0,

or, after developing the determinant, we need only prove that, for
p\in(0,f)
,

(B.24)

-g\left(g^{\prime\prime}(1-f^{-2}p^{2})+[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}\right)\neq 0.

From (
B.16
), we see that
g
is positive on
(0,f)
. Hence, we need to prove that, for
p\in(0,f)
,

(B.25)

g^{\prime\prime}(1-f^{-2}p^{2})+[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}\neq 0.

Now, on the one hand, we recall that, thanks to (
B.19
),

(B.26)

[g-pg^{\prime}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-f^{-2}p^{2}}}\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}.

Moreover, as we have already used many times,

(B.27)

\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}=\partial_{1}G\partial_{\Sigma}p_{1},

where
\partial_{1}G>0
(see Lemma
2.8
), and
\partial_{\Sigma}p_{1}
is positive since we are on the upper right quadrant (see (
2.17
)). Overall, there holds that

(B.28)

[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}>0.

In particular, coming back to equation (
B.25
), we already see that, if

(B.29)

\forall p\in(0,f),\hskip 18.49988ptg^{\prime\prime}(p)\geqslant 0,

then we have proved the claim. Now, we recall that, thanks to Lemma
2.4
, there holds

(B.30)

g^{\prime}(I)=-\omega(I),

hence,

(B.31)

g^{\prime\prime}(I)=-\omega^{\prime}(I),

which is thus nonnegative if (
2.76
) is satisfied.

Now, since there holds, in the case of the round sphere,

(B.32)

g(I)\equiv 1\hskip 18.49988ptg^{\prime}(I)\equiv 0\hskip 18.49988ptg^{\prime\prime}(I)\equiv 0,

then we can expect that (
B.25
) still holds if
\omega^{\prime}
is not nonnegative but
\mathcal{S}
is close to the sphere in the sense that
\omega^{\prime}
is not too large. Indeed, if
\omega^{\prime}\geqslant 0
, there holds first

(B.33)

g^{\prime}(p)=-\omega(p)=-\int_{0}^{p}\omega^{\prime}(I)dI\leqslant 0.

Hence, for all
p\in(0,f)
, there holds

(B.34)

g^{\prime\prime}(1-f^{-2}p^{2})+[g-pg^{\prime}]f^{-2}\geqslant g(p)-\omega^{\prime}(p)>g(p)-1.

Now, observe that, from formula (
B.16
), there holds

(B.35)

g(1)\geqslant 1.

Since, moreover,
g^{\prime}\leqslant 0
, we thus find that, for all
p\in(0,f)
,

(B.36)

g(p)\geqslant g(1)\geqslant 1.

This ensures ultimately that (
B.25
) holds.

In order to conclude the proof, we need only prove that, if the curve
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+}
has an everywhere non zero curvature, then
\mathcal{D}:=\{q_{1}(\sigma,\xi)\leqslant 1\}
is strictly convex. Now, since
q_{1}
is even in both
\Theta
and
\Sigma
, thanks to Lemma
2.4
and to (
2.17
), the boundary of
\mathcal{D}
on each of the four quadrants is obtained from
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+}
by a rotation. Since
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
is smooth, there holds necessarily that the curvature of
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}^{+}
is in fact everywhere positive, and thus that the curvature of
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
is everywhere positive (except maybe at the four points of intersection with the axis), i.e. that
\mathcal{D}
is strictly convex.
∎

B.3
Discussion of the hypotheses

In this section, we briefly discuss Proposition
2.5
, in order to justify its hypotheses.

First, if
\omega^{\prime}(I)
is not bounded, it is possible that the set

(B.37)

\mathcal{D}:=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\hskip 18.49988ptq_{1}(x,\xi)\leqslant 1\}

is
not
convex, in particular when
x
is near the equator. Indeed, in the case of a very thin oblong surface of revolution, i.e., with the Definition
2.6
, in the case where
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{E}(1,b)
with
b\gg 1
, one can prove rigorously that, if
x
is on the equator, then the set
\mathcal{D}
looks like the left figure in Figure
31
provided
b
is large enough.

Now, the geometric meaning of this fact is that the bicharacteristic curves of
q_{1}
starting at
x_{0}\in\gamma_{E}
with near vertical initial direction
(x_{0},\xi)\in T_{x_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{S}
will at first turn "in the wrong direction". Precisely, let us choose a direction
\xi=(\Theta,\Sigma)
very close to the vertical direction, i.e.
0<\Theta<<\Sigma
. Then, locally, while the geodesic
t\mapsto P(\Phi_{t}(x_{0},\xi))
starts by turning to the right, (see the black curve in Figure
32
), i.e.
\dot{\theta}>0
, the bicharacteristic curve
t\mapsto P(\Phi^{q_{1}}_{t}(x_{0},\xi))
starts by turning to the left, (see the red curve in Figure
32
), i.e.
\dot{\theta}<0
. This can be computed by the explicit formula for the Hamiltonian flow (
2.10
).

Figure 32:
Intersection of bicharacteristic curves for an oblong ellipsoid of revolution

However, after some time, when the bicharacteristic curve
t\mapsto P(\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x_{0},\xi))
reaches high latitudes (i.e.
\sigma
is close to
\frac{L}{2})
), its velocity vector rotates, and it eventually turns to the right, as depicted in Figure
32
(red curve). In particular, as one can see on this figure, the bicharacteristic curves necessarily intersects the meridian passing through
x_{0}
outside of the antipodal point
\bar{x_{0}}
. Hence, if the set
\mathcal{D}
is
not
convex, there are always some exceptional intersections of bicharacteristic curves.

This phenomenon can be interpreted by the explicit equation for the bicharacteristics of
q_{1}
given by Lemma
2.5
. Indeed, find
0<I\ll 1
the Clairaut integral of
(x_{0},\xi)
. Then, the bicharacteristic
t\mapsto\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x_{0},\xi)
is given by

(B.38)

t\mapsto\Phi_{\frac{t}{2\pi}\tau(I)}\circ\Phi^{p_{2}}_{-t\omega(I)}(x_{0},\xi).

Now, since the geodesic
t\mapsto\Phi_{\frac{t}{2\pi}\tau(I)}(x_{0},\xi)
is very close to a meridian, it starts by turning very slowly to the right, i.e.
\dot{\theta}
is initially very small, as seen on Figure
32
(black curve). On the contrary, the influence of the rotation of angle
-t\omega(I)
takes over since
\omega(I)
is very large. Hence, even if the total phase shift along the bicharacteristic curve
t\mapsto P(\Phi_{t}^{q_{1}}(x_{0},\xi))
is exactly
\pi>0
after a time
t=\pi
, the phase shift starts by being nonpositive for
0<t\ll 1
. In particular, there is necessarily a time
0<t<\pi
at which the phase shift is exactly
0
, which corresponds to an intersection with the meridian, as seen on Figure
32
.

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
11

Before turning to the proof of the Theorem, let us insist that, especially for Appendix
C.4
, we use very similar methods than in
[
1
]
, which was our main source of inspiration.

C.1
First reductions and the method of integration by parts

First, we start by writing

(C.1)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(\lambda)=\int_{I}dxe^{i\lambda\phi^{1D}(x)}\times\left(\int_{U}dye^{i\lambda(\phi_{x}(y)-\phi^{1D}(x))}\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)\right)\\
=:\int_{I}dxe^{i\lambda\phi^{1D}(x)}J(\lambda,x)\end{split}

Now, since
\phi^{1D}
satisfies the property
(VdC)_{p}
with constants
C,c
, we can apply Proposition
3.4
. We define

(C.2)

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\sup_{x}|J^{\prime}(\lambda,x)|=:A(\lambda)\\
&\displaystyle\sup_{x}|J(\lambda,x)|=:B(\lambda).\end{split}

Then, there holds thanks to Proposition
3.4
that

(C.3)

\mathcal{I}(\lambda)\lesssim(c\lambda)^{-\frac{1}{p}}\left(1+|I|\frac{C}{c}\right)\left(|I|A(\lambda)+B(\lambda)\right).

Hence, all amounts to bounding
A(\lambda)
and
B(\lambda)
. We start with reducing
A(\lambda)
to a similar form than
B(\lambda)
. Indeed, the problem is that, upon computing
J^{\prime}(\lambda,x)
, using that, by definition,

(C.4)

\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y(x))=0,

we find that

(C.5)

\begin{split}J^{\prime}(\lambda,x)&=\int dye^{i\lambda(\phi(x,y)-\phi(x,y(x)))}\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)\times i\lambda\left(\partial_{x}\phi(x,y)-\partial_{x}\phi(x,y(x))\right)\\
&+\int dye^{i\lambda(\phi(x,y)-\phi(x,y(x)))}\partial_{x}\left(\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)\right)\\
&=J_{1}(\lambda,x)+J_{2}(\lambda,x).\end{split}

Hence, there is a priori an undesirable power
\lambda
in
J_{1}
. Now, there holds that

(C.6)

\left(\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)(\partial_{x}\phi(x,y)-\partial_{x}\phi(x,y(x)))\right)(y=y(x))=0,

so the mains order term in the usual stationary phase Lemma
9
yields that

(C.7)

|J_{1}(\lambda,x)|=O_{\phi,\zeta,a}(\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}}).

However, the standard methods for obtaining quantitative upper bounds for an oscillatory integral of the form

(C.8)

\int dye^{i\lambda\Phi(y)}b(y)dy,

where
\Phi
has a nondegenerate stationary point
y_{0}
and

(C.9)

b(y)=O(|y-y_{0}|),

usually yield only a bound of the form

(C.10)

O(\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}),

see for example
[
71
]
[Equation (23), Chapter VIII].

The idea, to recover a full
\lambda^{-1}
, is to integrate by parts in
y
. Indeed, assume for a moment that there exists a smooth vector field
v(x,y)
such that

(C.11)

\partial_{x}\phi(x,y)-\partial_{x}\phi(x,y(x))=v(x,y)\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y).

Then, we may absorb the
\lambda
by integrating by parts : there holds

(C.12)

\begin{split}J_{1}(\lambda,x)&=\int dy\left(v\cdot\nabla_{y}\right)\left(e^{i\lambda(\phi(x,y)-\phi(x,y(x)))}\right)\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)\\
&=-\int dye^{i\lambda(\phi(x,y)-\phi(x,y(x)))}\nabla_{y}\cdot\left(\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)v(x,y)\right),\end{split}

to which we can apply usual stationary phase analysis to find a quantitative
O(\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}})
bound.

C.2
The vector field of the integration by parts
v(x,y)

Now, we turn to the construction of such a vector field
v
. Let us first write

(C.13)

\begin{split}\partial_{x}\phi(x,y)-\partial_{x}\phi(x,y(x))&=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(\partial_{x}\nabla_{y}\phi\right)(x,y(x)+t(y-y(x)))dt\right)\cdot(y-y(x))\\
&=:w(x,y)\cdot(y-y(x)).\end{split}

We observe moreover that, using (
C.4
), there holds

(C.14)

\begin{split}\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y)&=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(\nabla_{y}^{2}\phi\right)(x,y(x)+t(y-y(x)))dt\right)(y-y(x))\\
&=:M(x,y)(y-y(x)).\end{split}

In particular, with the Definition
3.6
,

(C.15)

M(x,y(x))=\nabla_{y}^{2}\phi(x,y(x))=H(x).

As a consequence, for
y
close to
y(x)
,
M(x,y)
is invertible. Indeed, we recall the following standard lemma.

Lemma C.1

(Neumann series)
.

Let
A\in GL_{d}(\mathbb{C})
and
B\in\mathcal{M}_{d}(\mathbb{C})
. Assume that

(C.16)

\|A-B\|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|A^{-1}\|^{-1}

for any operator norm
\|\cdot\|
. Then there holds

(C.17)

\begin{split}B&\in GL_{d}(\mathbb{C})\\
\text{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\|B^{-1}\|&\lesssim\|A^{-1}\|.\end{split}

Now, with the Notation
3.3
, observe that

(C.18)

\|M(x,y)-H(x)\|\leqslant\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{3,3}(\phi)|y-y(x)|.

Hence, if

(C.19)

|y-y(x)|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{3,3}^{(y)}(\phi)\|H(x)^{-1}\|\right)^{-1},

then there holds, using Lemma
C.1
,

(C.20)

M(x,y)\in GL_{d}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 18.49988pt\text{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\|M(x,y)^{-1}\|\lesssim\|H(x)^{-1}\|\lesssim N(\phi).

Now, condition (
C.19
) is
always
satisfied for
(x,y)\in supp(\zeta)
by definition of
\zeta
. Hence, on
supp(\zeta)
, we may write

(C.21)

y-y(x)=M(x,y)^{-1}\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y),

and hence, using (
C.13
), and the symmetry of
M^{-1}(x,y)
, there holds

(C.22)

\begin{split}\partial_{x}\phi(x,y)-\partial_{x}\phi(x,y(x))&=w(x,y)\cdot\left(M^{-1}(x,y)\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y)\right)\\
&=\left(M^{-1}(x,y)w(x,y)\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y).\end{split}

Hence, (
C.11
) holds with

(C.23)

v(x,y):=M^{-1}(x,y)w(x,y).

We now give bounds on the derivatives of
v(x,y)
. We find that

(C.24)

\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,\ell}(v)\lesssim\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}=\ell}\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,m_{1}}(w)\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,m_{2}}(M^{-1}).

Now, on the one hand, it is obvious using (
C.13
) that

(C.25)

\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,m}(w)\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{1,m+1}(\partial_{x}\phi).

On the other hand, a quick induction argument yields that the following (rough !) bound holds

(C.26)

\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,m}(M^{-1})\lesssim\|M(x,y)^{-1}\|^{m+1}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(M)\right)^{m}.

Using the definition of
M
(
C.14
), there holds

(C.27)

\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(M)\lesssim\mathcal{M}_{2,m+2}^{(y)}(\phi).

Hence, using (
C.27
) and (
C.20
), there holds

(C.28)

\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,m}(M^{-1})\lesssim\mathcal{N}(\phi)^{m+1}\left(\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{2,m+2}(\phi)\right)^{m}.

Thus, we ultimately find that

(C.29)

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,\ell}(v)&\lesssim\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}=\ell}\mathcal{N}(\phi)^{m_{1}+1}\left(\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{2,m_{1}+2}(\phi)\right)^{m_{1}}\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{1,m_{2}+1}(\partial_{x}\phi)\\
&\lesssim\mathcal{M}_{1,\ell+1}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{\ell+1}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,l+2}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{\ell}.\end{split}

C.3
Uniform model
\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x)
for the oscillatory integral which is to bound

Now, thanks to the construction of
v(x,y)
, we can write
J_{1}(\lambda,x)
in the form

(C.30)

J_{1}(\lambda,x)=e^{-i\lambda\phi(x,y(x)))}\int dye^{i\lambda\phi(x,y)}b_{1}(x,y),

where

(C.31)

b_{1}(x,y):=\nabla_{y}\cdot(\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)v(x,y)).

In particular, thanks to (
C.29
), there holds

(C.32)

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{0,\ell}^{(y)}(b_{1})&\lesssim\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=\ell+1}\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{1}}^{(y)}(\zeta)\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{2}}^{(y)}(a)\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{3}}^{(y)}(v)\\
&\lesssim\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=\ell+1}\left(\mathcal{M}_{3,3}^{(y)}(\phi)\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{2}}(a)\mathcal{M}_{1,m_{3}+1}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m_{3}+1}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,m_{3}+2}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m_{3}}\\
&\lesssim\mathcal{M}_{0,\ell+1}(a)\mathcal{M}_{1,\ell+2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{\ell+2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,\ell+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{\ell+1}.\end{split}

Similarly, one can write

(C.33)

J_{2}(\lambda,x)=e^{-i\lambda\phi(x,y(x)))}\int dye^{i\lambda\phi(x,y)}b_{2}(x,y),

where

(C.34)

\begin{split}b_{2}(x,y)&=\partial_{x}(\zeta(x,y)a(x,y))\\
&=\partial_{x}\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)+\zeta(x,y)\partial_{x}a(x,y).\end{split}

Since
\zeta(x,\cdot)
is a smooth localizer inside a ball of center
y(x)
and of radius
\sim\left(\mathcal{M}_{3,3}^{(y)}(\phi)N(\phi)\right)^{-1}
, one can further bound

(C.35)

\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\zeta)\lesssim\left(\displaystyle\sup_{x}|y^{\prime}(x)|\right)\left(\mathcal{M}_{3,3}^{(y)}(\phi)N(\phi)\right)^{m+1}.

Now, differentiating in
x
the equation (
C.4
), there holds

(C.36)

y^{\prime}(x)=-H(x)^{-1}(\partial_{x}\nabla_{y}\phi)(x,y(x)).

Hence there holds

(C.37)

|y^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim\mathcal{N}(\phi)\mathcal{M}_{1,1}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi).

Thus, bounding roughly, there holds

(C.38)

\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(b_{2})\lesssim\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m+2}\mathcal{M}_{1,1}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{M}_{3,3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right).

Finally,

(C.39)

J(\lambda,x)=e^{-i\lambda\phi(x,y(x))}\int e^{i\lambda\phi(x,y)}\zeta(x,y)a(x,y)

where

(C.40)

\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(\zeta a)\lesssim\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\mathcal{M}_{3,3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m}\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(a).

Hence, bounds (
C.32
), (
C.38
) and (
C.40
) yield that we need only bound

(C.41)

\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x):=\int dye^{i\lambda\phi(x,y)}b(y),

where

(C.42)

\mathcal{M}_{0,m}(b)\lesssim\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,m+1}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,m}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,m+2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m+2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,m+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m+1},

and where
b(y)
is supported in the ball
\mathcal{B}(x)
defined by (
3.185
).

C.4
Bounding
\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x)

Now, let
\varepsilon>0
be a small constant, let
\chi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a smooth bump function such that
\chi\equiv 1
on the ball
B(0,1)
and
\chi=0
outside of
B(0,2)
. Consider the following partition of unity

(C.43)

1=\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y))+(1-\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y)))=:\chi_{1}(y)+\chi_{2}(y),

so that, in particular, for all two integer
k\geqslant 0
, and for
i=1,2
,

(C.44)

\mathcal{M}_{0,k}^{(y)}(\chi_{i})\lesssim\varepsilon^{-k}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,k}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{k}.

Then, we can divide
\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x)
into two parts, namely

(C.45)

\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x)=\int dye^{i\lambda\phi(x,y)}b(y)\chi_{1}(y)+\int dye^{i\lambda\phi(x,y)}b(y)\chi_{2}(y)=:\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda,x)+\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda,x).

On the one hand, in order to bound
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda,x)
, let us introduce the change of variable

(C.46)

w:=H(x)(y-y(x)),

where we recall that
H(x)
is defined by Definition
3.6
. Then, using moreover the fact that, by the definition of
M
(
C.14
), there holds

(C.47)

\nabla_{y}\phi(x,y)=M(x,y)(y-y(x)),

we may write

(C.48)

\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda,x)=|\det H(x)|^{-1}\int e^{i\lambda\phi(x,y(w))}\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}M(x,y(w))H(x)^{-1}w)b(H(x)^{-1}w)dw.

Hence, bounding by the absolute value, there holds

(C.49)

|\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda,x)|\leqslant|\det H(x)|^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{(y)}(b)\int\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}M(x,y(w))H(x)^{-1}w).

Now, observe that, thanks to (
C.20
), there holds

(C.50)

|M(x,y(w))H(x)^{-1}w|\geqslant\left\|H(x)M(x,y(w))^{-1}\right\|^{-1}|w|\gtrsim|w|.

In particular,
w\mapsto\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}M(x,y(w))^{-1}w)H(x)^{-1}w)
is supported inside a ball

(C.51)

\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\hskip 18.49988pt|w|\lesssim\varepsilon\}.

Hence, from (
C.49
), we find that

(C.52)

\begin{split}|\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda,x)|&\lesssim|\det H(x)|^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{(y)}(b)\varepsilon^{d}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon^{d}|\det H(x)^{-1}|\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,1}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,0}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{2}\mathcal{M}_{2,3}^{(y)}(\phi)\end{split}

On the other hand, in order to bound
\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda,x)
, we observe that, on the support of
\chi_{2}
, we can integrate by parts in
y
, since
\nabla_{y}\phi
doesn’t vanish. Indeed, define the smooth vector field

(C.53)

X:=\frac{\nabla_{y}\phi\cdot\nabla_{y}}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{2}},

such that

(C.54)

e^{i\lambda\phi}=\frac{1}{i\lambda}X(e^{i\lambda\phi}).

Then, there holds, for any integer
K\geqslant 1

(C.55)

\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda,x)=\frac{1}{(i\lambda)^{K}}\int e^{i\lambda\phi}\left(X^{*}\right)^{K}\left(\chi_{2}b\right),

where
X^{*}
is the adjoint of
X
, namely

(C.56)

X^{*}f(y)=-\nabla_{y}\cdot\left(\frac{\nabla_{y}\phi}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{2}}f\right)=-\frac{\left(\nabla_{y}\phi\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla_{y}^{2}\phi\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{4}}f-\frac{\nabla_{y}\phi\cdot\nabla_{y}f}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{2}}.

A tedious induction, based on this formula, yields that, for all
K\geqslant 1
, and for all function
f(y)
,

(C.57)

\left|\left(X^{*}\right)^{K}f(y)\right|\lesssim\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}=K}\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{1}}(f)\frac{\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{2}+1}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m_{2}}}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{K+m_{2}}}.

In particular, applying this last formula to
f=\chi_{2}b
, and using the a priori estimates on
\chi_{2}
(
C.44
), and on
b
(
C.42
), there holds

(C.58)

\begin{split}\left|\left(X^{*}\right)^{K}\left(\chi_{2}b\right)\right|&\lesssim\chi_{2}\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=K}\varepsilon^{-m_{1}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{1}}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m_{1}}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m_{2}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,m_{2}+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{m_{2}}\frac{\left(\mathcal{M}^{(y)}_{0,m_{3}+1}(\phi)\right)^{m_{3}}}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{K+m_{3}}}\\
&\times\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,K+1}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,K}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,K+2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{2}\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\\
&\lesssim\chi_{2}\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=K}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m_{1}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m_{2}}|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{K+m_{3}}}\\
&\times\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,K+1}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,K}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,K+2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{K+1}.\end{split}

Using, again, the change of variable (
C.46
), there holds that, for
K>d
,

(C.59)

\int\chi_{2}\frac{dy}{|\nabla_{y}\phi|^{K+m_{3}}}\lesssim|\det H(x)|^{-1}\varepsilon^{-K-m_{3}+d},

from which we deduce that

(C.60)

\begin{split}|\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda,x)|&\lesssim\lambda^{-K}\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=K}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{m_{1}}\varepsilon^{-K-m_{2}-m_{3}+d}\\
&\times\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,K+1}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,K}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,K+2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{K+1}|\det H(x)|^{-1}\\
&\lesssim\lambda^{-K}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{K+1}\left(\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{K}\varepsilon^{-K+d}+\varepsilon^{-2K+d}\right)\\
&\times\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,K+1}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,K}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,K+2}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{2}|\det H(x)|^{-1}.\end{split}

Now, comparing the bound on
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda,x)
(
C.52
) and on
\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda,x)
(
C.60
), we are able to choose
\varepsilon
optimally, depending on the dominant term. It is natural to impose that

(C.61)

\max\left(\lambda^{-K}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{K}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{K}\varepsilon^{-K},\lambda^{-K}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{K}\varepsilon^{-2K}\right)=1.

In particular, we see that the term in the max which gives the condition for
\varepsilon
depends on whether or not there holds

(C.62)

\mathcal{N}(\phi)\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This motivates hypothesis (
3.187
) in the theorem. With this hypothesis, we may thus choose, with
K=d+1
,

(C.63)

\varepsilon=\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,d+4}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and ultimately find the bound

(C.64)

|\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x)|\lesssim\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,d+4}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}|\det H(x)|^{-1}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,d+2}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,d+1}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,d+3}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi).

In particular,
A(\lambda)
and
B(\lambda)
defined by (
C.2
) satisfy similar bounds, and, this, coming back to estimate (
C.3
), concludes the proof of the theorem.

Remark C.1
.

If the technical hypothesis (
3.187
) doesn’t hold, i.e. if, for some
K
,

(C.65)

\mathcal{N}(\phi)\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,K+3}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geqslant\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}},

then one can still concludes the proof, but the winning term in the
\max
(
C.61
) is not always the second one. Hence, one needs to choose

(C.66)

\varepsilon=\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{2,d+4}^{(y)}(\phi)\mathcal{N}(\phi).

Overall, this would yield the bound

(C.67)

|\mathcal{J}(\lambda,x)|\lesssim\lambda^{-d}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,d+4}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{d+1}|\det(H(x))|^{-1}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{d+2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,d+2}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,d+1}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right)\mathcal{M}_{1,d+3}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi),

and, ultimately, the conclusion of the theorem should be changed to

(C.68)

|\mathcal{I}(\lambda)|\lesssim_{d}\lambda^{-d-\frac{1}{p}}c^{-\frac{1}{p}}\left(1+|I|\left(1+\frac{C}{c}\right)\right)^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}(\phi)\right)^{-1}\left(\mathcal{N}(\phi)\right)^{d+2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{2,d+4}^{(y)}(\phi)\right)^{d+1}\\
\times\mathcal{M}_{1,d+3}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}\phi)\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,d+2}^{(y)}(a)+\mathcal{M}_{0,d+1}^{(y)}(\partial_{x}a)\right).

While the exponent
\lambda^{-d}
in this upper bound seems artificially a better bound than the usual
\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}}
, the bound is worse because of (
C.65
).

Appendix D
Study of the bicharacteristic length function

In this section, we give some useful technical results on the bicharacteristic length function
\psi
defined by Definition
2.10
. Precisely, we study the function

(D.1)

d(\sigma,t):=\psi((t,\sigma),(0,\sigma)),

which is defined for
t\in S^{1}
and
\sigma\in\left(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\right)
, and smooth if moreover
t\neq 0
, thanks to Lemma
2.7
.

D.1
Away from the equator

In this section, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma D.1
.

The function
d(\sigma,t)
defined by (
D.1
) satisfies

(D.2)

\forall\sigma\left(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\right)\backslash\{0\},\ \forall t\in(-\pi,\pi)\backslash\{0\},\hskip 18.49988pt\partial_{tt}d(\sigma,t)\neq 0.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we fix
\sigma\in\left(0,\frac{L}{2}\right)
, and we do the proof for
t\in(0,\pi)
. Let us denote

(D.3)

\xi(t):=-\nabla_{x}\psi((t,\sigma),(0,\sigma)).

Thanks to the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we know that, on the one hand,

(D.4)

q_{1}(\sigma,\xi(t))=1,

i.e.
\xi(t)\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
(see (
4.11
)). On the other hand, we also know, thanks to this proof, that
\xi(t)
is the direction of the unique bicharacteristic curve of
q_{1}
joining
(t,\sigma)
to
(0,\sigma)
in time
s(t)=d(\sigma,t)
by definition. We first claim

(D.5)

\forall t\in(0,\pi),\ \frac{d}{dt}\xi(t)\neq 0.

Indeed, let us fix
t\in(0,\pi)
, and let us introduce, as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, the wavefront set at time
s(t)
of the bicharacteristic flow of
q_{1}
starting at
(0,\sigma)
, i.e.

(D.6)

Q:=\{x\in\mathcal{S}\ \text{such that}\ \psi(x,(0,\sigma))=s(t)\}=\{P(\Phi_{s(t)}^{q_{1}}(0,\sigma,\xi)),\ \xi\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\}.

Then, by definition,
x=(t,\sigma)\in Q
, and, around
x
,
Q
is a smooth curve, whose normal at
x
is
-\xi(t)
. In particular, if
\delta t
is an infinitesimal, we see that the bicharacteristic curve joining
(0,\sigma)
to
(t+\delta t,\sigma)
passes through
Q
at a point
x+\delta x
such that

(D.7)

\delta x=\delta t\sin(\alpha)+O((\delta t)^{2}),

where
\alpha
is the angle between
-\xi(t)
and the horizontal direction, as can be seen on Figure
33
.

Figure 33:
The wavefront
Q
around
x

Thus, because the map

(D.8)

\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\mapsto P(\Phi_{s(t)}^{q_{1}})\in Q

is locally around
(\xi(t))
a smooth bijection, we can thus conclude that the direction of the bicharacteristic curve joining
(0,\sigma)
to
(t+\delta t,\sigma)
is
-\xi(t)-\delta\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
such that

(D.9)

-\delta\xi\ \text{is proportional to}\ \left(\delta t\sin(\alpha)+O((\delta t)^{2})\right),

or, in other words, that

(D.10)

\frac{d}{dt}\xi(t)\ \text{is proportional to}\ -\sin(\alpha).

To conclude the proof of (
D.5
), there only remains to observe that
\sin(\alpha)\neq 0
, because
\sigma\neq 0
and
t\neq 0
hence the direction
\xi(t)
cannot be horizontal.

Now, since, again,
\xi(t)
is not horizontal, then the projection on the first coordinate

(D.11)

\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\mapsto q_{2}(\sigma,\xi)

is locally around
\xi(t)
a smooth bijection (see for example the arguments of Paragraph
4.2.2
). Thus, (
D.3
) and (
D.5
) yields ultimately that

(D.12)

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\partial_{\theta}\psi((t,\sigma),(0,\sigma))\right)\neq 0,

or, in other words, that

(D.13)

\partial_{tt}d(\sigma,t)\neq 0.

∎

D.2
Near the equator

In this section, we give the following local expansion of the function
d
around the equator
\sigma=0
. We will give the main steps of the proof, without detailing all the computations.

Lemma D.2
.

There exists a constant
C
depending only on
\mathcal{S}
such that

(D.14)

\partial_{t}d(\sigma,t)=1-C\sigma^{2}f(\sigma,t)^{2},

where
f(\sigma,t)
is a smooth function on
\left(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\right)\times\left((-\pi,\pi)\backslash\{0\}\right)
such that

(D.15)

f(0,t)=\tan\left(\frac{t}{2}\right).

Proof.

We give the proof for
\sigma\geqslant 0
and
t\in(0,\pi)
. Using similar notations than for the proof of Lemma
D.1
, we define, for
|\sigma|<<1
and
t\in(0,\pi)
,

(D.16)

\xi(\sigma,t)\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}

the direction of the unique bicharacteristic joining
(0,\sigma)
to
(t,\sigma)
in a time
s(\sigma,t)\in(0,\pi)
(observe that there is a sign difference with the definition of
\xi(t)
in the proof of Lemma
D.1
). As we have already argued in this proof, there holds

(D.17)

\partial_{t}d(\sigma,t)=q_{2}(\sigma,\xi(\sigma,t)).

Now, let us write

(D.18)

\xi(\sigma,t)=(\Theta(\sigma,t),\Sigma(\sigma,t))\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}.

Since
|\sigma|<<1
, we know a priori that
|\Sigma(\sigma,t)|<<1
. Hence, there holds (see Notation
4.1
)

(D.19)

\partial_{t}d(\sigma,t)=\Theta(\sigma,t)=|g_{\sigma}(0)|\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Sigma(\sigma,t)\right)^{2}+O\left(\left(\Sigma(\sigma,t)\right)^{4}\right)\right).

Now, let us study the bicharacteristic of
q_{1}
starting at
x=(0,\sigma)
with direction
\xi=(\Theta,\Sigma)\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
such that

(D.20)

0<\Sigma\lesssim\sigma.

We denote

(D.21)

\Phi_{s}^{q_{1}}(x,\xi)=(\theta(t),\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t)).

Using, for example, Lemma
2.5
, one can prove that, for all times,

(D.22)

|(\sigma(t),\Sigma(t))|\lesssim\sigma.

Using the explicit formula for the Hamiltonian flow (
2.10
), we know that there holds along this bicharacteristic

(D.23)

\dot{\sigma}(t)=\partial_{\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t))=\left(\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),0)\right)\Sigma(t)+O\left(\Sigma(t)^{2}\right).

Using similar techniques than in Appendix
A.1
, one can prove moreover that

(D.24)

\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),0)=\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)+O\left((\sigma(t)+\Sigma(t))^{2}\right),

where
\Theta_{0}
is such that

(D.25)

g_{0}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}\Theta_{0}\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Overall, using moreover (
D.22
), one can prove that

(D.26)

\dot{\sigma}(t)=\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)\Sigma(t)+O(\sigma^{2}).

Now, one can rigorously prove that this equality can be differentiated, and that there holds

(D.27)

\ddot{\sigma}(t)=\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)\dot{\Sigma}(t)+O(\sigma^{2}).

Thanks to (
2.10
), we know that

(D.28)

\dot{\Sigma}(t)=-\partial_{\sigma}q_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t)).

Now, using the representation of Lemma
2.4
, there holds

(D.29)

\partial_{\sigma}q_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t))=(\partial_{1}G)(p_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t)),\Theta(t))\partial_{\sigma}p_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t)).

There holds, using (
2.17
) and (
D.22
),

(D.30)

\partial_{\sigma}p_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t))=\frac{-\frac{\Theta(t)^{2}}{f(\sigma)^{3}}f^{\prime}(\sigma)}{p_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t))}=\Theta_{0}|f^{\prime\prime}(0)|\sigma+O(\sigma^{2}).

Moreover, there holds similarly

(D.31)

(\partial_{1}G)(p_{1}(\sigma(t),\Theta(t),\Sigma(t)),\Theta(t))=\partial_{1}G(1,1)+O(\sigma^{2}).

Overall, we thus find that

(D.32)

\begin{split}\ddot{\sigma}(t)&=\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)\dot{\Sigma}(t)+O(\sigma^{2})\\
&=-\left(\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)\partial_{1}G(1,1)\Theta_{0}|f^{\prime\prime}(0)|\right)\sigma+O(\sigma^{2}).\end{split}

Now, using similar techniques than the one used in Appendix
A
, one can prove that

(D.33)

\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)\partial_{1}G(1,1)\Theta_{0}|f^{\prime\prime}(0)|=1.

Finally, we see that
\sigma(t)
solves the differential equation

(D.34)

\ddot{\sigma}(t)+\sigma(t)=O(\sigma^{2}).

Integrating explicitly this equation, and since the time interval is compact, there holds, for some constants
\alpha,\beta
.

(D.35)

\sigma(t)=\alpha\cos(t)+\beta\sin(t)+O(\sigma^{2}).

In order to find
\alpha
and
\beta
, we may use the fact

(D.36)

\begin{split}&\sigma(0)=\sigma\\
&\dot{\sigma}(0)=(\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0))\Sigma+O(\sigma^{2}).\end{split}

Hence, we find that

(D.37)

\begin{split}&\alpha=\sigma+O(\sigma^{2})\\
&\beta=c^{-1}\Sigma+O(\sigma^{2}),\end{split}

where

(D.38)

c:=\partial_{\Sigma\Sigma}q_{1}(0,\Theta_{0},0)\neq 0.

Now, if we choose
\Sigma=\Sigma(\sigma,t)
(see (
D.18
)), then, by definition,

(D.39)

\sigma(t)=\sigma.

This equation, along with the representation (
D.35
), yields the following equation for
\Sigma(\sigma,t)

(D.40)

\sigma\cos(t)+c^{-1}\Sigma(\sigma,t)\sin(t)+O(\sigma^{2})=\sigma.

We thus find that

(D.41)

\Sigma(\sigma,t)=c\sigma\frac{1-\cos(t)}{\sin(t)}+O\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sin(t)}\right).

Observe that, on any compact subset of
(0,\pi)
,
\frac{1}{\sin(t)}
is bounded. Hence, as long as we restrict locally to a compact subset of
(0,\pi)
, we may write

(D.42)

\Sigma(\sigma,t)=c\sigma\frac{1-\cos(t)}{\sin(t)}+O(\sigma^{2}).

Using that

(D.43)

\frac{1-\cos(t)}{\sin(t)}=\tan\left(\frac{t}{2}\right),

we may further reduce to

(D.44)

\Sigma(\sigma,t)=c\sigma\tan\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+O(\sigma^{2}),

where the remainder is locally uniform on any compact subset of
(0,\pi)
. Coming back to equation (
D.19
), we finally find that

(D.45)

\partial_{t}d(\sigma,t)=|g_{\sigma}(0)|\left(1-\frac{1}{2}c^{2}\sigma^{2}\tan^{2}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+0(\sigma^{4})\right).

In order to finally complete the proof of the Lemma, there only remains to observe that

(D.46)

|g_{\sigma}(0)|=1+O(\sigma^{2}).

∎
